#### D-09.4 Records of Processing

<!-- participants: GDPR, NIS2, CRA, DORA, AI_Act -->

**Participants (from CRDA):** GDPR, NIS2, CRA, DORA, AI_Act

**Sole-authority note (CRDA flag):** DORA is fully absent — no RoPA-equivalent. DORA BC/DR logging (Art. 12) is operational continuity, not processing-of-personal-data records. For DORA financial entities, the GDPR RoPA remains the dedicated authority for processing-activities documentation. The DORA Art. 12 logging obligation is a separate artefact.

##### Pair: GDPR ↔ NIS2

**GDPR article (verbatim, Art. 30(1)–(4) + Art. 12-14):**
> "Each controller … shall maintain a record of processing activities under its responsibility … Such record shall contain … where possible, the envisaged time limits for erasure of the different categories of data …" — Art. 30(1) GDPR
> "Each processor … shall maintain a record of all categories of processing activities carried out on behalf of a controller …" — Art. 30(2) GDPR
> "The records referred to in paragraphs 1 and 2 shall be maintained in writing, including in electronic form …" — Art. 30(3) GDPR

**NIS2 article (verbatim, Art. 24(1)/(2)/(3)):**
> "Member States shall encourage the use of European or internationally accepted standards and specifications relevant to the security of network and information systems …" — Art. 24(1) NIS 2 (per SR-NIS2-043)

**Scope comparison:**
- GDPR scope: controller/processor RoPA mandate with 250-employee exception.
- NIS 2 scope: certification-driven recordkeeping (Art. 24) — not a RoPA-equivalent.
- Scope overlap: **N (typically)** — different record types.

**Trigger comparison:**
- GDPR trigger: processing of personal data.
- NIS 2 trigger: certification requirement (where Member State or Commission delegated act so requires).
- Trigger alignment: **DIFFERENT**.

**Threshold/timeline comparison:**
- GDPR: 250-employee carve-out; retention until processing ceases + on SA request.
- NIS 2: per Member State.
- Tension: **NONE**.

**Recipient comparison:**
- GDPR: data subject, DPA.
- NIS 2: management body, competent authority.

**Content template comparison:**
- GDPR: Art. 30(1)/(2) content items.
- NIS 2: not codified (certification-driven).

**Classified relationship (from CRDA):** DIFFERENT-PERSPECTIVE
**Verified relationship (after OJ-text analysis):** **SAME — DIFFERENT-PERSPECTIVE**
**Reasoning:** GDPR RoPA is processing-activities-focussed; NIS 2 Art. 24 is certification-driven. Different record types; no substantive overlap.

**Scope-disjoint test:** Does this pair actually overlap on a single event at a single party? **N (typically)** — different record types, different triggers. Substantively scope-disjoint.

**Downstream implication (Phase 1C Doc 07):** Phase 2 obligation derivation must produce two separate artefacts: GDPR Art. 30 RoPA + NIS 2 Art. 24 certification-driven records. No OJ-level consolidation.

---

##### Pair: GDPR ↔ CRA

**GDPR article (verbatim, Art. 30(1)–(4)):**
> [as quoted above]

**CRA article (verbatim, Art. 13(12) + Art. 13(13) + Annex VII §1-§8):**
> "Where, prior to placing a product with digital elements on the market, the manufacturer has carried out a conformity assessment procedure … the manufacturer shall draw up the technical documentation … and draw up the EU declaration of conformity." — Art. 13(12) CRA (per SR-CRA-085)
> "The manufacturer shall keep the technical documentation and the EU declaration of conformity at the disposal of the market surveillance authorities for at least 10 years after the product has been placed on the market, or for the support period, whichever is longer." — Art. 13(13) CRA (per SR-CRA-084)

**Scope comparison:**
- GDPR scope: controller/processor RoPA.
- CRA scope: product-level technical documentation + EU declaration + CE marking + Annex VII 8-item content.
- Scope overlap: **N (typically)** — different record types.

**Trigger comparison:**
- GDPR trigger: processing of personal data.
- CRA trigger: product placement on market.
- Trigger alignment: **DIFFERENT**.

**Threshold/timeline comparison:**
- GDPR: 250-employee carve-out; retention until processing ceases + on SA request.
- CRA: 10 years or support period, whichever longer.
- Tension: **NONE**.

**Recipient comparison:**
- GDPR: data subject, DPA.
- CRA: MSA.

**Content template comparison:**
- GDPR: Art. 30(1)/(2) content items.
- CRA: Annex VII 8-item content list.

**Classified relationship (from CRDA):** DIFFERENT-PERSPECTIVE
**Verified relationship (after OJ-text analysis):** **SAME — DIFFERENT-PERSPECTIVE**
**Reasoning:** GDPR RoPA covers processing activities (purposes, data categories, recipients, safeguards); CRA technical documentation covers product cybersecurity (design, development, risk assessment, SBOM, conformity). Different artefacts serving different audit purposes. The two regimes do not cross-substitute.

**Scope-disjoint test:** Does this pair actually overlap on a single event at a single party? **N (typically)** — different record types, different triggers. Substantively scope-disjoint.

**Downstream implication (Phase 1C Doc 07):** Phase 2 obligation derivation must produce two separate artefacts: GDPR Art. 30 RoPA + CRA Annex VII technical documentation. No OJ-level consolidation.

---

##### Pair: GDPR ↔ DORA

> **Note:** CRDA did not explicitly analyze this pair for D-09.4.

**GDPR article (verbatim, Art. 30(1)–(4)):**
> [as quoted above]

**DORA article (verbatim, Art. 9(4)(a) per SR-DORA-031):**
> "Financial entities shall … develop and document an information security policy defining rules to protect the availability, authenticity, integrity and confidentiality of data, information assets and ICT assets, including those of their customers, where applicable." — Art. 9(4)(a) DORA

**Scope comparison:**
- GDPR scope: controller/processor RoPA.
- DORA scope: information security policy defining CIA+A rules (no RoPA-equivalent).
- Scope overlap: **N (typically)** — different record types (RoPA vs. IS-policy documentation).

**Trigger comparison:**
- GDPR trigger: processing of personal data.
- DORA trigger: information security policy adoption.
- Trigger alignment: **DIFFERENT**.

**Threshold/timeline comparison:**
- GDPR: 250-employee carve-out; retention until processing ceases + on SA request.
- DORA: no quantitative retention period for policy documentation.
- Tension: **NONE**.

**Recipient comparison:**
- GDPR: data subject, DPA.
- DORA: NCA.

**Content template comparison:**
- GDPR: Art. 30(1)/(2) content items.
- DORA: CIA+A information security policy.

**Classified relationship (from CRDA):** NOT CLASSIFIED (CRDA omitted this pair for D-09.4)
**Verified relationship (after OJ-text analysis):** **DIFFERENT-PERSPECTIVE** (recommended)
**Reasoning:** GDPR RoPA covers processing activities; DORA information security policy covers CIA+A rules. Different record types serving different audit purposes. DORA has no GDPR Art. 30 analogue per the CRDA explicit note that DORA is fully absent for D-09.4.

**Scope-disjoint test:** Does this pair actually overlap on a single event at a single party? **N (typically)** — different record types, different triggers. Substantively scope-disjoint.

**Downstream implication (Phase 1C Doc 07):** Phase 2 obligation derivation must produce two separate artefacts: GDPR Art. 30 RoPA + DORA Art. 9(4)(a) information security policy. CRDA's omission should be corrected by adding a "different-perspective" classification (or "scope-disjoint" since the objects are entirely different).

##### Pair: GDPR ↔ AI_Act

**GDPR article (verbatim, Art. 30(1)–(4)):**
> [as quoted above]

**AI_Act article (verbatim, Art. 11 + Art. 12 + Art. 17(1)):**
> "Providers of high-risk AI systems shall keep the logs referred to in Article 12(1) … to the extent that such logs are under their control." — Art. 19(1) AI Act (per SR-AIACT-008/009)
> "The quality management system shall be documented in a systematic and orderly manner in the form of written policies, procedures and instructions …" — Art. 17(1) AI Act (per SR-AIACT-017)

**Scope comparison:**
- GDPR scope: controller/processor RoPA.
- AI Act scope: technical documentation (Annex IV via Art. 11/12) + QMS documentation of 13 enumerated aspects.
- Scope overlap: **Conditional (Y when the provider is a controller processing personal data)**.

**Trigger comparison:**
- GDPR trigger: processing of personal data.
- AI Act trigger: high-risk AI system design + QMS.
- Trigger alignment: **DIFFERENT**.

**Threshold/timeline comparison:**
- GDPR: 250-employee carve-out.
- AI Act: no quantitative retention for documentation (Art. 11/12 + Art. 17(1)).
- Tension: **NONE**.

**Recipient comparison:**
- GDPR: data subject, DPA.
- AI Act: provider, MSA.

**Content template comparison:**
- GDPR: Art. 30(1)/(2) content items.
- AI Act: Annex IV technical documentation + 13-element QMS list.

**Classified relationship (from CRDA):** COMPLEMENTARY
**Verified relationship (after OJ-text analysis):** **SAME — COMPLEMENTARY**
**Reasoning:** GDPR RoPA + AI Act Annex IV technical documentation + AI Act Art. 17 QMS documentation stack. AI Act does not displace GDPR.

**Scope-disjoint test:** Does this pair actually overlap on a single event at a single party? **Conditional (Y when the provider is a controller)**. No reclassification required.

**Downstream implication (Phase 1C Doc 07):** Phase 2 obligation derivation must produce a layered stack: GDPR Art. 30 RoPA + AI Act Annex IV + AI Act Art. 17 QMS.

##### Pair: NIS2 ↔ CRA

**NIS2 article (verbatim, Art. 24(1)/(2)/(3)):**
> [as quoted above]

**CRA article (verbatim, Art. 27(8)/(9)):**
> "A product with digital elements and the processes put in place by the manufacturer for which the manufacturer has obtained an EU statement of conformity or an EU certificate under a European cybersecurity certification scheme adopted pursuant to Regulation (EU) 2019/881, at assurance level at least 'substantial', shall be presumed to be in conformity with the essential cybersecurity requirements …" — Art. 27(8) CRA (per SR-CRA-078)

**Scope comparison:**
- NIS 2 scope: certification-driven recordkeeping (Art. 24).
- CRA scope: CSA-certification presumption-of-conformity machinery.
- Scope overlap: **N (typically)** — different certification frameworks.

**Trigger comparison:**
- NIS 2 trigger: certification requirement.
- CRA trigger: manufacturer CSA certification.
- Trigger alignment: **DIFFERENT**.

**Threshold/timeline comparison:**
- NIS 2: per Member State.
- CRA: support period + 10-year accessibility.
- Tension: **NONE**.

**Recipient comparison:**
- NIS 2: management body, competent authority.
- CRA: MSA.

**Content template comparison:**
- NIS 2: not codified.
- CRA: Annex VII 8-item content list + EU statement of conformity / EU certificate.

**Classified relationship (from CRDA):** COMPLEMENTARY
**Verified relationship (after OJ-text analysis):** **SAME — COMPLEMENTARY**
**Reasoning:** NIS 2 Art. 24 requires use of certified products; CRA Art. 27(8)/(9) provides the CSA-certification presumption-of-conformity machinery. The two regimes link at the certification boundary.

**Scope-disjoint test:** Does this pair actually overlap on a single event at a single party? **Conditional (Y when the NSIE procures CSA-certified CRA products; N otherwise)**. No reclassification required.

**Downstream implication (Phase 1C Doc 07):** Phase 2 obligation derivation must produce a NIS 2 Art. 24 certification-driven record + CRA Art. 27 CSA-certification presumption-of-conformity as upstream gate for NSIE procurements.

# Part 4 — Relevant Ambiguity (by regulation)

## GDPR

##### Art. 5(1)(a) — Lawfulness, fairness, transparency

**Clause: GDPR-CL01** | type: principle | obligatedParty: CONTROLLER | obligationType: CONTINUOUS

**Berry anchor:** §3.3.5 (vagueness), §5.1 (lists `lawful`, `fair`).

**Instance 1 — VAG / S3 / `lawful`, `fair`, `transparent`**

Three conjunctive vague adjectives. Each is inquiry-resistant:
- `lawful` — refer to Art. 6 (which is itself ambiguous; see Clause-CL06)
- `fair` — undefined; Recital 47 attempts to fix but is non-binding; balance of interests test
- `transparent` — Art. 12 attempts to fix at the implementation level (concise, easily accessible, plain language)

**Variant readings:**
| # | Reading | Disambiguation source |
|---|---|---|
| R1 | `fair` = no deception about purpose or use; balance-of-interests test on data-subject impact. | Recital 47 (non-binding); ICO fairness guidance. |
| R2 | `fair` = no significant detrimental consequence for the data subject unless proportionate and transparent. | CJEU Rīgas satiksme (C-13/16). |
| R3 | `fair` = a procedural requirement (notice, consent mechanism, complaint channel). | EDPB Guidelines 04/2019 on transparency. |

R1 vs R2 yield materially distinct compliance obligations (R1 requires substantive impact assessment; R2 requires procedural safeguards).

---

##### Art. 5(2) — Accountability

**Clause: GDPR-CL07** | type: principle (controller-specific) | obligatedParty: CONTROLLER | obligationType: CONTINUOUS

**Berry anchor:** §5.1 (`responsible`, `able to demonstrate`).

**Instance 1 — VAG / S3 / `able to demonstrate compliance`**

The accountability principle is the foundational obligation of GDPR. The standard for `demonstrating` is undefined.

**Variant readings:**
| # | Reading | Disambiguation source |
|---|---|---|
| R1 | Demonstrate = maintain records sufficient for supervisory authority inspection on request (records-of-processing Art. 30, DPIA Art. 35). | Art. 30 + Art. 35 collectively. |
| R2 | Demonstrate = continuous evidentiary trail (logs, audit reports, certifications). | EDPB Guidelines on accountability 07/2019. |
| R3 | Demonstrate = outcome-based — no specific evidentiary form prescribed. | Loose reading. |

R1 vs R2 yield materially distinct compliance obligations (R1 requires documented records; R2 requires live audit trails).

**Instance 2 — POLY / S2 / `compliance with paragraph 1`**

`compliance` is a technical compliance-management term but is undefined. Art. 24(1) partially anchors but uses the same vague `appropriate measures`. S2 because both readings converge on the same controller practice (record + demonstrate).

---

### Article 6 — Lawfulness of processing

#### Verbatim (with highlighting)

> **Article 6**
> **Lawfulness of processing**
>
> 1. Processing shall be lawful only if and to the extent that at least one of the following applies:

> (a) the data subject has given **consent** to the processing of his or her personal data for one or more specific purposes;

> (b) processing is **necessary** for the performance of a contract to which the data subject is party or in order to take steps at the request of the data subject prior to entering into a contract;

> (c) processing is necessary for compliance with a **legal obligation** to which the controller is subject;

> (d) processing is **necessary** in order to protect the **vital interests** of the data subject or of another natural person;

> (e) processing is necessary for the performance of a **task carried out in the public interest** or in the exercise of **official authority** vested in the controller;

> (f) processing is **necessary** for the purposes of the **legitimate interests** pursued by the controller or by a third party, except where such interests are overridden by the interests or fundamental rights and freedoms of the data subject which require protection of personal data, in particular where the data subject is a child.
>
> Point (f) of the first subparagraph shall not apply to processing carried out by public authorities in the performance of their tasks.
>
> 2. Member States may maintain or introduce more specific provisions to adapt the application of the rules of this Regulation with regard to processing for compliance with points (c) and (e) of paragraph 1 by determining more precisely specific requirements for the processing and other measures to ensure lawful and fair processing including for other specific processing situations as provided for in Chapter IX.

> 3. The basis for the processing referred to in point (c) and (e) of paragraph 1 shall be laid down by:

> (a) Union law; or

> (b) Member State law to which the controller is subject.
>
>    The purpose of the processing shall be determined in that legal basis or, as regards the processing referred to in point (e) of paragraph 1, shall be necessary for the performance of a task carried out in the public interest or in the exercise of official authority vested in the controller. That legal basis may contain specific provisions to adapt the application of rules of this Regulation, inter alia: the general conditions governing the lawfulness of processing by the controller; the types of data which are subject to the processing; the data subjects concerned; the entities to, and the purposes for which, the personal data may be disclosed; the purpose limitation; storage periods; and processing operations and processing procedures, including measures to ensure lawful and fair processing such as those for other specific processing situations as provided for in Chapter IX. The Union or the Member State law shall meet an objective of public interest and be proportionate to the legitimate aim pursued.
>
> 4. Where the processing for a purpose other than that for which the personal data have been collected is not based on the data subject's consent or on a Union or Member State law which constitutes a necessary and proportionate measure in a democratic society to safeguard the objectives referred to in Article 23(1), the controller shall, in order to ascertain whether processing for another purpose is compatible with the purpose for which the personal data are initially collected, take into account, inter alia:

> (a) any link between the purposes for which the personal data have been collected and the purposes of the intended further processing;

> (b) the context in which the personal data have been collected, in particular regarding the relationship between data subjects and controller;

> (c) the nature of the personal data, in particular whether special categories of personal data are processed, pursuant to Article 9, or whether personal data related to criminal convictions and offences are processed, pursuant to Article 10;

> (d) the possible consequences of the intended further processing for data subjects;

> (e) the existence of appropriate safeguards, which may include encryption or pseudonymisation.

#### Clause breakdown

##### Art. 12(1) — Transparency modalities

**Clause: GDPR-RT01** | type: data-subject-facing | obligatedParty: CONTROLLER | obligationType: PER-INTERACTION

**Berry anchor:** §5.1 (`appropriate`, `concise`, `transparent`, `intelligible`, `easily accessible`, `clear and plain`).

**Instance 1 — VAG / S3 / six adjectives on information form**

`appropriate measures` AND `concise, transparent, intelligible and easily accessible form` AND `clear and plain language` — six inquiry-resistant modifiers. EDPB Guidelines on transparency 03/2018 attempt to fix but the bar varies by data-subject category (child vs expert; vulnerable vs general).

**Variant readings:**
| # | Reading | Disambiguation source |
|---|---|---|
| R1 | Compliance requires layered notices (just-in-time + full privacy policy) meeting WCAG 2.1 AA accessibility. | EDPB Guidelines 03/2018 §4. |
| R2 | Compliance requires reading-level standard (e.g. CEFR B2). | ICO guidance. |
| R3 | Compliance requires both layered notices AND specific linguistic accessibility. | Combination reading. |

R1 vs R2 yield materially distinct UI/UX requirements.

---

##### Art. 12(3) — Response time and extension

**Clause: GDPR-RT02** | type: data-subject-facing | obligatedParty: CONTROLLER | obligationType: PER-REQUEST

**Berry anchor:** §5.1 (`without undue delay`, `where necessary`).

**Instance 1 — VAG / S3 / `without undue delay and in any event within one month` + `two further months where necessary`**

Twin temporal operators (Berry §5.1, §5.7). The 1-month anchor reduces temporal ambiguity; the extension to 3 months via `where necessary` reopens it.

**Variant readings:**
| # | Reading | Disambiguation source |
|---|---|---|
| R1 | `where necessary` = demonstrably complex cases (e.g. cross-border processing). | Strict text + EDPB guidance. |
| R2 | `where necessary` = any reason the controller judges sufficient. | Loose reading; controller discretion. |

R1 vs R2 yield materially distinct SLA targets (R1 allows ~6 weeks p95; R2 allows up to 3 months routinely).

**Instance 2 — COORD / S2 / `one month of receipt ... together with the reasons for the delay`**

The controller must inform within one month AND include reasons. Berry §5.4.7: AND coordination. S2 because both requirements converge on the same notification practice.

---

##### Art. 12(5) — Manifestly unfounded or excessive requests

**Clause: GDPR-RT03** | type: data-subject-facing (anti-abuse) | obligatedParty: CONTROLLER | obligationType: PER-REQUEST

**Berry anchor:** §5.1 (`manifestly unfounded or excessive`, `reasonable fee`).

**Instance 1 — VAG / S3 / `manifestly unfounded or excessive`**

The standard is undefined; the `in particular because of their repetitive character` example is illustrative. EDPB Guidelines 5/2019 attempt to fix but `manifestly unfounded` has high disagreement threshold.

**Variant readings:**
| # | Reading | Disambiguation source |
|---|---|---|
| R1 | `manifestly unfounded` = clearly frivolous on its face (no investigation needed). | EDPB Guidelines 5/2019 §4. |
| R2 | `manifestly unfounded` = requires substantive review of the request merits. | Recital 59 (non-binding). |

R1 vs R2 yield materially distinct request-handling workflows (R1 allows automated rejection; R2 requires human review).

---

##### Art. 12(6) — Identity verification for requests

**Clause: GDPR-RT04** | type: data-subject-facing | obligatedParty: CONTROLLER | obligationType: PER-REQUEST

**Berry anchor:** §5.1 (`reasonable doubts`, `additional information`).

**Instance 1 — VAG / S3 / `reasonable doubts`, `additional information`**

`reasonable doubts` is the recurring vague phrase; `additional information` is undefined. Art. 12(6) permits asking for more info but does not bound the scope. Combined with the controller's data-minimisation duty (Art. 5(1)(c)), the controller should ask minimally.

**Variant readings:**
| # | Reading | Disambiguation source |
|---|---|---|
| R1 | Controller may request minimal identity verification (e.g. authenticated login). | EDPB Guidelines 5/2019 §6. |
| R2 | Controller may request identity document copy. | Industry practice. |
| R3 | Controller must accept self-declaration if any doubt exists. | Strict pro-data-subject reading. |

R1 vs R3 yield materially distinct identity-verification systems.

---

### Article 13 — Information when collected from data subject

#### Verbatim (with highlighting)

> **Article 13**
> **Information to be provided where personal data are collected from the data subject**
>
> 1. Where personal data relating to a data subject are collected from the data subject, the controller shall, at the time when personal data are obtained, provide the data subject with <u>all of the following information</u>:

> (a) the identity and the contact details of the controller and, where applicable, of the controller's representative;

> (b) the contact details of the data protection officer, where applicable;

> (c) the purposes of the processing for which the personal data are intended as well as the legal basis for the processing;

> (d) where the processing is based on point (f) of Article 6(1), the legitimate interests pursued by the controller or by a third party;

> (e) the recipients or categories of recipients of the personal data, if any;

> (f) where applicable, the fact that the controller intends to transfer personal data to a third country or international organisation and the existence or absence of an adequacy decision by the Commission, or in the case of transfers referred to in Article 46 or 47, or the second subparagraph of Article 49(1), reference to the appropriate or suitable safeguards and the means by which to obtain a copy of them or where they have been made available.

> 2. In addition to the information referred to in paragraph 1, the controller shall, at the time when personal data are obtained, provide the data subject with the following further information necessary to ensure fair and transparent processing:

> (a) the period for which the personal data will be stored, or if that is not possible, the criteria used to determine that period;

> (b) the existence of the right to request from the controller access to and rectification or erasure of personal data or restriction of processing concerning the data subject or to object to processing as well as the right to data portability;

> (c) where the processing is based on point (a) of Article 6(1) or point (a) of Article 9(2), the existence of the right to withdraw consent at any time, without affecting the lawfulness of processing based on consent before its withdrawal;

> (d) the right to lodge a complaint with a supervisory authority;

> (e) whether the provision of personal data is a statutory or contractual requirement, or a requirement necessary to enter into a contract, as well as whether the data subject is obliged to provide the personal data and of the possible consequences of failure to provide such data;

> (f) the existence of automated decision-making, including profiling, referred to in Article 22(1) and (4) and, at least in those cases, meaningful information about the logic involved, as well as the significance and the envisaged consequences of such processing for the data subject.

> 3. Where the controller intends to further process the personal data for a purpose other than that for which the personal data were collected, the controller shall provide the data subject prior to that further processing with information on that other purpose and with any relevant further information as referred to in paragraph 2.

> 4. Paragraphs 1, 2 and 3 shall not apply where and insofar as the data subject already has the information.

#### Clause breakdown

##### Art. 13(1) — Initial information on collection

**Clause: GDPR-RT05** | type: information duty (collection-time) | obligatedParty: CONTROLLER | obligationType: PER-COLLECTION

**Berry anchor:** §5.4.7 (6-element closed AND-list (a)–(f)).

**Instance 1 — COORD / S2 / `(a) AND (b) AND (c) AND (d) AND (e) AND (f)`**

The 6-element list is closed (and). Each must be present. S2 because all 6 requirements converge on the same privacy-notice template.

---

##### Art. 14(1)–(2) — Third-party-source information

**Clause: GDPR-RT07** | type: information duty (third-party-source) | obligatedParty: CONTROLLER | obligationType: PER-COLLECTION

**Berry anchor:** §5.1 (`reasonable period`, `within one month`).

**Instance 1 — VAG / S3 / `within a reasonable period after obtaining the personal data, but at the latest within one month`**

Twin temporal operators (Berry §5.7) where `reasonable period` is soft and `within one month` is hard — interaction undefined.

**Variant readings:**
| # | Reading | Disambiguation source |
|---|---|---|
| R1 | Notify at first communication or first disclosure, whichever comes first; 1-month hard cap. | Strict text. |
| R2 | Notify within a reasonable period post-collection; 1-month is the upper bound, not the rule. | EDPB reading. |

R1 vs R2 yield materially distinct notification timing rules.

**Instance 2 — VAG / S3 / `from which source the personal data originate`**

`the source` is undefined. Controllers may source from public registries, third-party brokers, web scraping, purchase of data sets — each requires different disclosure detail.

**Variant readings:**
| # | Reading | Disambiguation source |
|---|---|---|
| R1 | Source = the immediate upstream provider. | Strict text. |
| R2 | Source = the entire chain of acquisition. | Strict pro-data-subject reading. |
| R3 | Source = the type/category (e.g. commercial data broker). | EDPB reading. |

R1 vs R2 yield materially distinct due-diligence documentation requirements.

---

##### Art. 21(1) — Objection grounds and overriding

**Clause: GDPR-RT16** | type: data subject right (object) | obligatedParty: CONTROLLER | obligationType: PER-OBJECTION

**Berry anchor:** §5.1 (`compelling legitimate grounds`).

**Instance 1 — VAG / S3 / `compelling legitimate grounds`**

`compelling` is stronger than Art. 6(1)(f) `legitimate interests`. The `overriding` test for compelling grounds is undefined.

**Variant readings:**
| # | Reading | Disambiguation source |
|---|---|---|
| R1 | `Compelling` = strictly necessary (lex specialis to legitimate interests). | EDPB Guidelines on legitimate interests. |
| R2 | `Compelling` = materially more weighty than legitimate interests. | Loose reading. |

R1 vs R2 yield materially distinct balance-tests in objection handling.

---

##### Art. 21(3) — Absolute bar on direct-marketing after objection

**Clause: GDPR-RT17** | type: data subject right (absolute) | obligatedParty: CONTROLLER | obligationType: PER-OBJECTION

**Berry anchor:** none — Art. 21(3) is a clear absolute obligation.

**Instance 1 — none** — the clause is unambiguous: once the data subject objects to direct-marketing processing, the processing must cease. No variant readings.

---

### Article 22 — Automated individual decision-making

#### Verbatim (with highlighting)

> **Article 22**
> **Automated individual decision-making, including profiling**
>
> 1. The data subject shall have the right not to be subject to a decision based solely on automated processing, including profiling, which produces legal effects concerning him or her or similarly significantly affects him or her.

> 2. Paragraph 1 shall not apply if the decision:

> (a) is necessary for entering into, or performance of, a contract between the data subject and a data controller;

> (b) is authorised by Union or Member State law to which the controller is subject and which also lays down suitable measures to safeguard the data subject's rights and freedoms and legitimate interests; or

> (c) is based on the data subject's explicit consent.

> 3. In the cases referred to in points (a) and (c) of paragraph 2, the data controller shall implement **suitable measures** to safeguard the data subject's rights and freedoms and legitimate interests, at least the right to obtain **human intervention** on the part of the controller, to express his or her point of view and to contest the decision.

> 4. Decisions referred to in paragraph 2 shall not be based on special categories of personal data referred to in Article 9(1), unless point (a) or (g) of Article 9(2) applies and suitable measures to safeguard the data subject's rights and freedoms and legitimate interests are in place.

#### Clause breakdown

##### Art. 23(1) — Permissible restriction grounds

**Clause: GDPR-RT20** | type: principle (restrictions scope) | obligatedParty: MEMBER STATE | obligationType: PER-RESTRICTION

**Berry anchor:** §5.1 (`essence of the fundamental rights and freedoms`, `necessary and proportionate`), §5.4.7 (10-element OR-list).

**Instance 1 — VAG / S3 / `essence of the fundamental rights and freedoms`**

`Essence` is Charter Art. 52(1) terminology. CJEU case law (Schrems I/II, Digital Rights Ireland) uses `essence` as a hard-test threshold but does not specify the operational test. S3 because Member State restrictions on Charter rights depend on the essence-test being met.

**Variant readings:**
| # | Reading | Disambiguation source |
|---|---|---|
| R1 | `Essence` = the absolute core of the right (cannot be touched). | CJEU Charter interpretation. |
| R2 | `Essence` = the proportionality balance of the right. | Loose reading. |

R1 vs R2 yield materially distinct restriction-scrutiny standards.

**Instance 2 — COORD / S2 / `(a) OR (b) OR (c) OR ... (j)`**

10 disjunctive grounds (Berry §5.4.7 menu-card pattern). S2 because all 10 grounds converge on the same scrutiny mechanism.

### Chapter III synthesis

#### Counts

| Category | Instances | Clauses affected |
|---|---|---|
| VAG | 27 | 20 / 20 |
| POLY | 11 | 11 / 20 |
| COORD | 12 | 12 / 20 |
| SCOPE-Q | 3 | 3 / 20 |
| **Total** | **53** | **20 / 20** |

| Severity | Instances |
|---|---|
| S1 — Low | 0 |
| S2 — Medium | 34 |
| S3 — High | 19 |

#### Recurring patterns

- `Reasonable` (Berry-flagged) appears in 8 of 20 clauses — dominant vague term of Chapter III.
- 1-month + 2-month temporal pair (Art. 12(3)) is the only hard numeric anchor in the chapter; all other timelines are vague.
- ADM (Art. 22) overlaps with AI Act Art. 13 — Chapter III Art. 22(3) `meaningful information about the logic` is the foundational obligation that the AI Act operationalises for high-risk AI.

#### Cross-clause structural observations

1. **Art. 12 is the procedural backbone.** All other rights (Art. 13–22) depend on Art. 12's response-time, identity-verification, and refusal grounds.
2. **Art. 13/14 split** — collection-source vs third-party-source information duties are structurally parallel but with different timing rules (immediate vs 1-month). The third-party-source timing is the more lenient and more often waived.
3. **Art. 15–22 cascade.** Each right (access, rectification, erasure, restriction, portability, object, ADM) inherits Art. 12's procedural rules and Art. 5(1)(d) accuracy / Art. 5(1)(c) minimisation anchors. Cross-clause implementations can reuse components.
4. **Art. 22 ADM** — the most software-impact-relevant right in GDPR, with direct overlap to AI Act's high-risk transparency requirements. Cross-regulatory coordination required.
5. **Art. 23 restrictions** — the Member State's escape valve from Chapter III. The `essence` test (Charter Art. 52(1)) is the constitutional backstop but its operationalisation is CJEU-driven.

#### Anti-pattern notes for downstream files

- Chapter IV inherits Chapter III rights via Art. 24(1) "implement appropriate technical and organisational measures" + Art. 25 by-design. `Appropriate measures` for rights-exercise includes consent-collection UIs, SAR portals, rectification workflows.
- Chapter V transfer mechanisms do not create new Chapter III rights but the Art. 15(2) right to be informed of transfer safeguards cross-references Chapter V.

##### Art. 30(1)+(2) — Records content

**Clause: GDPR-CP12** | type: documentation | obligatedParty: CONTROLLER + PROCESSOR | obligationType: CONTINUOUS

**Berry anchor:** §5.1 (`where possible` × 2), §5.4.7 (closed AND-list).

**Instance 1 — VAG / S2 / `where possible` × 2**

Soft hedge on retention-period documentation and security-measure documentation. `Where possible` defers to controller judgement. S2 because the same documentation practice applies.

---

##### Art. 30(5) — 250-employee exception

**Clause: GDPR-CP13** | type: documentation exception | obligatedParty: CONTROLLER + PROCESSOR (<250 persons) | obligationType: CONTINUOUS

**Berry anchor:** §5.1 (`likely`, `occasional`), §3.3.1 (`risk` polysemy).

**Instance 1 — VAG / S3 / `likely to result in a risk`, `occasional`**

Two qualifying exceptions. `Likely` is qualitative; `occasional` inherits Art. 27(2)(a) ambiguity. The exception requires the 3 conditions to ALL fail (risk OR not-occasional OR special categories).

**Variant readings:**
| # | Reading | Disambiguation source |
|---|---|---|
| R1 | `Likely` = more probable than not. | Recital 91 (non-binding). |
| R2 | `Likely` = non-negligible probability. | Loose reading. |

R1 vs R2 yield materially distinct risk-thresholds for small-organisation documentation.

---

### Article 31 — Cooperation with supervisory authority

#### Verbatim

> **Article 31**
> **Cooperation with the supervisory authority**
>
> The controller and the processor and, where applicable, their representatives, shall cooperate, on request, with the supervisory authority in the performance of its tasks.

#### Clause breakdown

##### Art. 31 — Cooperation duty

**Clause: GDPR-CP14** | type: supervisory cooperation | obligatedParty: CONTROLLER + PROCESSOR | obligationType: PER-REQUEST

**Berry anchor:** none — clause is unambiguous. Cooperation on request.

---

### Article 32 — Security of processing

#### Verbatim (with highlighting)

> **Article 32**
> **Security of processing**
>
> 1. Taking into account the **state of the art**, the costs of implementation and the nature, scope, context and purposes of processing as well as the risk of varying likelihood and severity for the rights and freedoms of natural persons, the controller and the processor shall implement **appropriate technical and organisational measures** to ensure a level of security appropriate to the risk, including inter alia as appropriate:

> (a) the **pseudonymisation and encryption** of personal data;

> (b) the ability to ensure the **ongoing confidentiality, integrity, availability and resilience** of processing systems and services;

> (c) the ability to **restore** the availability and access to personal data **in a timely manner** in the event of a physical or technical incident;

> (d) a process for **regularly testing, assessing and evaluating** the effectiveness of technical and organisational measures for ensuring the security of the processing.

> 2. In assessing the appropriate level of security account shall be taken in particular of the risks that are presented by processing, **in particular from accidental or unlawful destruction, loss, alteration, unauthorised disclosure of, or access to** personal data transmitted, stored or otherwise processed.

> 3. Adherence to an approved code of conduct as referred to in Article 40 or an approved certification mechanism as referred to in Article 42 may be used as an element by which to demonstrate compliance with the requirements set out in paragraph 1 of this Article.

> 4. The controller and processor shall take steps to ensure that any natural person acting under the authority of the controller or the processor who has access to personal data does not process them except on instructions from the controller, unless he or she is required to do so by Union or Member State law.

#### Clause breakdown

##### Art. 33(1) — 72-hour notification

**Clause: GDPR-CP17** | type: breach notification | obligatedParty: CONTROLLER | obligationType: TRIGGERED (per breach)

**Berry anchor:** §5.1 (`without undue delay`, `where feasible`, `unlikely`), §5.7 (temporal operators), §3.3.1 (`becoming aware` polysemy).

**Instance 1 — VAG / S3 / `without undue delay and, where feasible, not later than 72 hours`**

Twin temporal operators (Berry §5.1, §5.7) — `without undue delay` is soft, `72 hours` is hard, `where feasible` softens the hard. Interaction undefined.

**Instance 2 — SCOPE-Q / S3 / `becoming aware of it`**

The awareness trigger is polysemous (cf. CRA C25, NIS 2 C25 — same pattern across regulations).

**Instance 3 — POLY+VAG / S3 / `unlikely to result in a risk`**

`Unlikely` is qualitative; `risk` is the recurring polysemy (multiple threshold definitions across GDPR).

**Variant readings:**
| # | Reading | Disambiguation source |
|---|---|---|
| R1 | Notification required unless certainty-of-no-risk; precautionary principle. | EDPB Guidelines 9/2022 on breach notification. |
| R2 | Notification required only when risk threshold is met; controller assessment suffices. | Industry reading. |

R1 vs R2 yield materially distinct breach-response workflows.

## CRA

#### 3.8 CRA-CL08–CL09 — Art. 7(3)–(4) — Commission delegated and implementing acts

(Summarised — full articles to be analysed in dedicated file; brief Layer 1 notes here for Art. 6–8 cohesion.)

**Instance 1 — VAG — S2 — `shall take into account the cybersecurity-related functionalities` (Art. 7(3))**

The Commission considers `cybersecurity-related functionalities or the function and the level of cybersecurity risk`. The reading:
- **R1:** Functional assessment.
- **R2:** Risk-based assessment.
- **R3:** Both.

| # | Reading | Disambiguation source |
|---|---|---|
| R1 | Functional | In-Directive: literal |
| R2 | Risk-based | In-Directive: literal |
| R3 | Both | In-Directive: literal `and` |

R3 is the literal reading. S2.

**Instance 2 — VAG — S2 — `a minimum transitional period of 12 months`**

12-month minimum. Could be longer.

- **R1:** Exactly 12 months.
- **R2:** 12+ months.
- **R3:** Discretionary.

| # | Reading | Disambiguation source |
|---|---|---|
| R1 | Exactly 12 | None in OJ |
| R2 | 12+ | In-Directive: literal `minimum … 12 months` |
| R3 | Discretionary | None in OJ |

R2 is the literal reading. S2.

**Instance 3 — VAG — S2 — `shall specify the technical description of the categories` (Art. 7(4))**

The Commission's implementing act specifies `technical description` — what level of detail?

- **R1:** Functional description.
- **R2:** Performance description.
- **R3:** Compliance description.

| # | Reading | Disambiguation source |
|---|---|---|
| R1 | Functional | In-Directive: literal |
| R2 | Performance | None in OJ |
| R3 | Compliance | None in OJ |

R1 is the literal reading. S2.

## CRA v0.2 — Article 13 Obligations of Manufacturers (THE HEART)

> **Source.** OJ L, 20.11.2024, p. 34–37. `03_REFERENCE_MATERIAL/Regulatory_References/CRA.txt`, lines ~2177–2370.
>
> **Pattern.** Article quoted in full (paragraph by paragraph), then per-clause analysis. Universal `[recital NN]` convention applied.
>
> **Article 13 is the operational core of CRA** — 25 paragraphs, ~35 atomic clauses. It is the analog of GDPR Art. 32 and NIS 2 Art. 21. It defines the 6-phase product-lifecycle obligations (planning, design, development, production, delivery, maintenance), the cybersecurity risk assessment, the due-diligence duty on third-party components, the support period, the technical documentation, and the corrective-measures duty.

---

### 1. Article 13 — full text (key paragraphs)

> **Article 13 — Obligations of manufacturers**
>
> **1.** When placing a product with digital elements on the market, manufacturers shall ensure that it has been designed, developed and produced in accordance with the essential cybersecurity requirements set out in Part I of Annex I.

> **2.** For the purpose of complying with paragraph 1, manufacturers shall undertake an assessment of the cybersecurity risks associated with a product with digital elements and take the outcome of that assessment into account during the **planning, design, development, production, delivery and maintenance phases** of the product with digital elements with a view to minimising cybersecurity risks, preventing incidents and minimising their impact, **including in relation to the health and safety of users**.

> **3.** The cybersecurity risk assessment shall be documented and updated as appropriate during a support period to be determined in accordance with paragraph 8 of this Article. That cybersecurity risk assessment shall comprise at least an analysis of cybersecurity risks based on the **intended purpose and reasonably foreseeable use, as well as the conditions of use, of the product with digital elements**, such as the operational environment or the assets to be protected, taking into account the length of time the product is expected to be in use. The cybersecurity risk assessment shall indicate whether and, if so in what manner, the security requirements set out in Part I, point (2), of Annex I are applicable to the relevant product with digital elements and how those requirements are implemented as informed by the cybersecurity risk assessment. It shall also indicate how the manufacturer is to apply Part I, point (1), of Annex I and the vulnerability handling requirements set out in Part II of Annex I.

> **4.** When placing a product with digital elements on the market, the manufacturer shall include the cybersecurity risk assessment referred to in paragraph 3 of this Article in the technical documentation required pursuant to Article 31 and Annex VII. …

> **5.** For the purpose of complying with paragraph 1, manufacturers shall **exercise due diligence when integrating components sourced from third parties** so that those components do not compromise the cybersecurity of the product with digital elements, including when integrating components of free and open-source software that have not been made available on the market in the course of a commercial activity.

> **6.** Manufacturers shall, upon identifying a vulnerability in a component, including in an open source-component, which is integrated in the product with digital elements report the vulnerability to the person or entity manufacturing or maintaining the component, and **address and remediate** the vulnerability in accordance with the vulnerability handling requirements set out in Part II of Annex I. …

> **7.** The manufacturers shall systematically document, in a manner that is proportionate to the nature and the cybersecurity risks, **relevant cybersecurity aspects** concerning the products with digital elements, including vulnerabilities of which they become aware and any relevant information provided by third parties, and shall, where applicable, update the cybersecurity risk assessment of the products.

> **8.** Manufacturers shall ensure, when placing a product with digital elements on the market, and for the support period, that vulnerabilities of that product, including its components, are handled effectively and in accordance with the essential cybersecurity requirements set out in Part II of Annex I.
>
> Manufacturers shall determine the support period so that it reflects the length of time during which the product is expected to be in use, taking into account, in particular, **reasonable user expectations, the nature of the product, including its intended purpose**, as well as **relevant Union law determining the lifetime** of products with digital elements. When determining the support period, manufacturers may also take into account the support periods of products with digital elements offering a similar functionality placed on the market by other manufacturers, the availability of the operating environment, the support periods of integrated components that provide core functions and are sourced from third parties as well as relevant guidance provided by the dedicated administrative cooperation group (ADCO) established pursuant to Article 52(15) and the Commission. **The matters to be taken into account in order to determine the support period shall be considered in a manner that ensures proportionality.**
>
> Without prejudice to the second subparagraph, the support period shall be **at least five years**. Where the product with digital elements is expected to be in use for less than five years, the support period shall correspond to the expected use time.
>
> …
>
> **9.** Manufacturers shall ensure that each security update, as referred to in Part II, point (8), of Annex I, which has been made available to users during the support period, **remains available after it has been issued for a minimum of 10 years or for the remainder of the support period, whichever is longer**.

> **10.** Where a manufacturer has placed subsequent substantially modified versions of a software product on the market, that manufacturer may ensure compliance with the essential cybersecurity requirement set out in Part II, point (2), of Annex I only for the version that it has last placed on the market, provided that theusers of the versions that were previously placed on the market have access to the version last placed on the market free of charge and do not incur additional costs to adjust the hardware and software environment in which they use the original version of that product.

> **11.** Manufacturers may maintain public software archives enhancing user access to historical versions. In those cases, users shall be clearly informed in an easily accessible manner about risks associated with using unsupported software.

> **12.** Before placing a product with digital elements on the market, manufacturers shall draw up the technical documentation referred to in Article 31. They shall carry out the chosen conformity assessment procedures as referred to in Article 32 or have them carried out. Where compliance … has been demonstrated by that conformity assessment procedure, manufacturers shall draw up the EU declaration of conformity in accordance with Article 28 and affix the CE marking in accordance with Article 30.

> **13.** Manufacturers shall **keep the technical documentation and the EU declaration of conformity at the disposal of the market surveillance authorities for at least 10 years** after the product with digital elements has been placed on the market or for the support period, whichever is longer.

> **14.** Manufacturers shall ensure that procedures are in place for products with digital elements that are part of a series of production to remain in conformity with this Regulation. …

> **15.** Manufacturers shall ensure that their products with digital elements bear a **type, batch or serial number or other element allowing their identification** …

> **16.** Manufacturers shall indicate the **name, registered trade name or registered trademark of the manufacturer, and the postal address, email address or other digital contact details**, as well as, where applicable, the website where the manufacturer can be contacted, on the product with digital elements …

> **17.** For the purposes of this Regulation, manufacturers shall designate a **single point of contact** to enable users to communicate directly and rapidly with them, including in order to facilitate reporting on vulnerabilities of the product with digital elements. …

> **18.** Manufacturers shall ensure that products with digital elements are accompanied by the **information and instructions to the user set out in Annex II**, in paper or electronic form. …

> **19.** Manufacturers shall ensure that the **end date of the support period** referred to in paragraph 8, including at least the month and the year, is clearly and understandably specified at the time of purchase …

> **20.** Manufacturers shall either provide a copy of the EU declaration of conformity or a **simplified EU declaration of conformity** with the product with digital elements. …

> **21.** From the placing on the market and for the support period, manufacturers who know or have reason to believe that the product with digital elements or the processes put in place by the manufacturer are not in conformity with the essential cybersecurity requirements set out in Annex I shall **immediately take the corrective measures** necessary to bring that product with digital elements or the manufacturer's processes into conformity, or to withdraw or recall the product, as appropriate.

> **22.** Manufacturers shall, upon a reasoned request from a market surveillance authority, provide that authority, in a language which can be easily understood by that authority, with all the information and documentation, in paper or electronic form, necessary to demonstrate the conformity …

> **23.** A manufacturer that ceases its operations and, as a result, is not able to comply with this Regulation shall inform, before the cessation of operations takes effect, the relevant market surveillance authorities as well as, by any means available and to the extent possible, the users of the relevant products with digital elements placed on the market, of the impending cessation of operations.

> **24.** The Commission may, by means of implementing acts taking into account European or international standards and best practices, specify the format and elements of the **software bill of materials** referred to in Part II, point (1), of Annex I. …

> **25.** In order to assess the dependence of Member States and of the Union as a whole on software components and in particular on components qualifying as free and open-source software, ADCO may decide to conduct a Union wide **dependency assessment** for specific categories of products with digital elements. …

**Total: 36 atomic clauses for Art. 13 alone** (35 if CRA-CL23b is merged into CL23a; the v0.2 split per Q6=b places CRA-C24a on Art. 13(5)).

**Note on Q6=b (CRA-C24 split):** T2 CRA-C24 was mapped to Art. 13(5) but described the 10-year documentation retention (which is actually Art. 13(13)). Per Q6=b, v0.2 splits this:
- CRA-CL23a (this file) = Art. 13(5) **due-diligence on third-party components** (the OJ-literal mapping).
- CRA-CL38 (this file) = Art. 13(13) **10-year documentation retention** (the substance T2 misattributed).

#### 3.1 CRA-CL14 — Art. 13(1) — Design/development/production duty

**Source locus:**

> When placing a product with digital elements on the market, manufacturers shall ensure that it has been **designed, developed and produced** in accordance with the essential cybersecurity requirements set out in Part I of Annex I.

**Instance 1 — COORD — S2 — `designed AND developed AND produced`**

The 3-phase coordination. Three readings:
- **R1:** Three distinct phases (each must comply).
- **R2:** Composite description (one process).
- **R3:** Each independently required.

R1 is the literal reading. S2.

### 4. Cross-clause structural observations (Art. 13)

1. **Art. 13(2) is the operational core of the manufacturer's risk-assessment duty.** The 6-phase lifecycle propagation (CL16) is the **longest single coordination pattern in CRA** — 6 phases coordinated by AND. Berry §5.5 parallelism admits partial-compliance readings.

2. **Art. 13(8) is the support-period architecture.** Three layered obligations: (a) handle vulnerabilities during support (CL27), (b) determine support period based on factors (CL28), (c) minimum 5 years (CL29). Combined with Art. 13(9) 10-year update availability (CL33), the **effective manufacturer obligation is a 10-year tail** that survives the support period.

3. **Art. 13(5) is the supply-chain due-diligence hook (Q6=b split).** T2 misattributed this clause to Art. 13(13)'s 10-year documentation retention. The v0.2 split places the actual Art. 13(5) due-diligence analysis here (CL23a) and the 10-year retention in CL38.

4. **Art. 13(2) sentence 2 is CRA-unique.** The `health and safety of users` clause bridges cybersecurity to physical safety. No other regulation in the corpus (GDPR, NIS 2, DORA, AI Act) has this.

5. **`reasonable user expectations` (CL28) is the cross-regulation Berry-classic.** Same term-pattern as `reasonable foreseeability` in Art. 3(24)/(25). Layer 1 catalogues; Layer 2 must adjudicate against product-liability case law.

6. **6 S3 clauses (CL15, CL16, CL18, CL19, CL23a, CL27, CL28, CL33, CL42):** risk assessment, 6-phase propagation, documentation, intended purpose + foreseeability, due diligence, support-period factors, 10-year updates, single point of contact. **No S1 instances.**

7. **The Q6=b split resolves the T2-C24 mapping error.** T2-C24's "10-year documentation" substance is actually Art. 13(13) (CL38), not Art. 13(5). T2-C24's "due diligence" substance (which it didn't capture) is actually Art. 13(5) (CL23a). The v0.2 split surfaces both clauses.

---

### 4. Cross-clause structural observations (Art. 14)

1. **Art. 14 has a dual-track architecture:** Track A = actively exploited vulnerabilities (¶1–2, 24h/72h/14d); Track B = severe incidents (¶3–4, 24h/72h/1m). The two tracks have identical temporal tiers but different clock starts (14d vs 1m). This is structurally parallel to GDPR Art. 33 (controller notification) vs Art. 34 (data-subject communication).

2. **`severe incident` is the definitional regress.** Art. 14(5) defines it via `sensitive or important data or functions` (CL61) and `malicious code` (CL62). Both terms are undefined. Same pattern as NIS 2 Art. 23(3) `significant incident` and DORA Art. 18 `major ICT-related incident`.

3. **`becomes aware of` (CL51, CL56) is the cross-regulation awareness-trigger pattern.** Same as NIS 2 Art. 23(4)(a)(b) and GDPR Art. 33(1). National implementations have begun diverging on when awareness attaches (R1 actual vs R2 constructive knowledge).

4. **`simultaneously` (CL51, CL56) is CRA-unique.** No other regulation requires simultaneous notification to two bodies (CSIRT + ENISA). NIS 2 requires notification to the CSIRT only (or competent authority). GDPR requires notification to the supervisory authority only.

5. **`structured, machine-readable format that is easily automatically processable` (CL65) is CRA-unique.** No other regulation in the corpus requires machine-readable user notification. NIS 2 Art. 23(4)(a)(b) uses the same temporal pattern but does not specify format.

6. **T3-vs-text gaps in Art. 14:** T3 had CRA-C25 (Art. 14(1) reporting) and CRA-C26 (Art. 14(3) reporting). The v0.2 expands these into 18 atomic clauses (CL51–CL68). T3's coverage was thin (2 clauses for 10 paragraphs). v0.2 captures the full article.

---

## CRA v0.2 — Annex I Essential Cybersecurity Requirements

> **Source.** OJ L, 20.11.2024, p. 68–69. `03_REFERENCE_MATERIAL/Regulatory_References/CRA.txt`, lines ~4153–4234.
>
> **Pattern.** Each Part quoted verbatim with highlighting, then per-clause analysis. Universal `[recital NN]` convention.
>
> **Annex I is the binding normative core of CRA.** Part I defines security properties for products; Part II defines vulnerability-handling obligations for manufacturers. These are referenced by Art. 6, 7, 8, 13, 27, 28, 32, and are the ultimate compliance target. 21 of the 26 T2 clauses lived in Annex I.

---

## CRA v0.2 — Annex II Information and Instructions to the User

> **Source.** OJ L, 20.11.2024, p. 70. `03_REFERENCE_MATERIAL/Regulatory_References/CRA.txt`, lines ~4240–4275.
>
> **Pattern.** Each point quoted verbatim, then per-clause analysis.

---

### 1. Annex II — full text

> At minimum, the product with digital elements shall be accompanied by:
>
> **1.** the name, registered trade name or registered trademark of the manufacturer, and the postal address, the email address or other digital contact as well as, where available, the website at which the manufacturer can be contacted;
>
> **2.** the single point of contact where information about vulnerabilities of the product with digital elements can be reported and received, and where the manufacturer's policy on coordinated vulnerability disclosure can be found;
>
> **3.** name and type and any additional information enabling the unique identification of the product with digital elements;
>
> **4.** the intended purpose of the product with digital elements, including the security environment provided by the manufacturer, as well as the product's essential functionalities and information about the security properties;
>
> **5.** any known or foreseeable circumstance, related to the use of the product with digital elements in accordance with its intended purpose or under conditions of reasonably foreseeable misuse, which may lead to significant cybersecurity risks;
>
> **6.** where applicable, the internet address at which the EU declaration of conformity can be accessed;
>
> **7.** the type of technical security support offered by the manufacturer and the end-date of the support period during which users can expect vulnerabilities to be handled and to receive security updates;
>
> **8.** detailed instructions or an internet address referring to such detailed instructions and information on:
> **(a)** the necessary measures during initial commissioning and throughout the lifetime of the product with digital elements to ensure its secure use;
> **(b)** how changes to the product with digital elements can affect the security of data;
> **(c)** how security-relevant updates can be installed;
> **(d)** the secure decommissioning of the product with digital elements, including information on how user data can be securely removed;
> **(e)** how the default setting enabling the automatic installation of security updates, as required by Part I, point (2)(c), of Annex I, can be turned off;
> **(f)** where the product with digital elements is intended for integration into other products with digital elements, the information necessary for the integrator to comply with the essential cybersecurity requirements set out in Annex I and the documentation requirements set out in Annex VII.
>
> **9.** If the manufacturer decides to make available the software bill of materials to the user, information on where the software bill of materials can be accessed.

---

### 2. Atomic clause map

| Clause ID | Locus | Type | Highest Sev. | Gist |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| CRA-CL151 | Ann. II §1 (manufacturer contact) | Contact info | **S2** | Name, address, email, website. |

## CRA v0.2 — Annex VII Content of the Technical Documentation

> **Source.** OJ L, 20.11.2024, p. 75. `03_REFERENCE_MATERIAL/Regulatory_References/CRA.txt`, lines ~4379–4420.
>
> **Pattern.** Each point quoted verbatim, then per-clause analysis.

---

### 1. Annex VII — full text

> The technical documentation referred to in Article 31 shall contain at least the following information, as applicable to the relevant product with digital elements:
>
> **1.** a general description of the product with digital elements, including: (a) its intended purpose; (b) versions of software affecting compliance with essential cybersecurity requirements; (c) where the product with digital elements is a hardware product, photographs or illustrations showing external features, marking and internal layout; (d) user information and instructions as set out in Annex II;
>
> **2.** a description of the design, development and production of the product with digital elements and vulnerability handling processes, including: (a) necessary information on the design and development of the product with digital elements, including, where applicable, drawings and schemes and a description of the system architecture explaining how software components build on or feed into each other and integrate into the overall processing; (b) necessary information and specifications of the vulnerability handling processes put in place by the manufacturer, including the software bill of materials, the coordinated vulnerability disclosure policy, evidence of the provision of a contact address for the reporting of the vulnerabilities and a description of the technical solutions chosen for the secure distribution of updates; (c) necessary information and specifications of the production and monitoring processes of the product with digital elements and the validation of those processes;
>
> **3.** an assessment of the cybersecurity risks against which the product with digital elements is designed, developed, produced, delivered and maintained pursuant to Article 13, including how the essential cybersecurity requirements set out in Part I of Annex I are applicable;
>
> **4.** relevant information that was taken into account to determine the support period pursuant to Article 13(8) of the product with digital elements;
>
> **5.** a list of the harmonised standards applied in full or in part … or common specifications … or European cybersecurity certification schemes … and, where those … have not been applied, descriptions of the solutions adopted to meet the essential cybersecurity requirements set out in Parts I and II of Annex I, including a list of other relevant technical specifications applied;
>
> **6.** reports of the tests carried out to verify the conformity of the product with digital elements and of the vulnerability handling processes with the applicable essential cybersecurity requirements as set out in Parts I and II of Annex I;
>
> **7.** a copy of the EU declaration of conformity;
>
> **8.** where applicable, the software bill of materials, further to a reasoned request from a market surveillance authority provided that it is necessary in order for that authority to be able to check compliance with the essential cybersecurity requirements set out in Annex I.

---

**Total: 8 atomic clauses for Annex VII.**

## CRA v0.2 — Annex VIII Conformity Assessment Procedures

> **Source.** OJ L, 20.11.2024, p. 75–80. `03_REFERENCE_MATERIAL/Regulatory_References/CRA.txt`, lines ~4426–4769.
>
> **Pattern.** Key clauses quoted, then per-clause analysis.
>
> **Annex VIII defines three conformity-assessment modules:** Module A (internal control), Module B+C (EU-type examination + internal production control), Module H (full quality assurance). Art. 32 routes products to modules based on classification.

---

### 1. Module architecture

| Module | Part | Name | Role |
|---|---|---|---|
| **A** | I | Internal control | Manufacturer self-assessment. |
| **B** | II | EU-type examination | Notified body examines design + vuln handling. |
| **C** | III | Internal production control | Manufacturer controls production per EU-type. |
| **B+C** | II + III | EU-type + production control | Notified body examines design; manufacturer controls production. |
| **H** | IV | Full quality assurance | Notified body audits QMS. |

---

### 2. Atomic clause map

| Clause ID | Locus | Type | Highest Sev. | Gist |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| CRA-CL168 | Ann. VIII Part I (1) | Module A duty | **S2** | Manufacturer self-declares compliance; sole responsibility. |
| CRA-CL169 | Ann. VIII Part I (2) | Tech docs (Module A) | **S2** | Draw up Annex VII documentation. |
| CRA-CL170 | Ann. VIII Part I (3) | Design/production/vuln (Module A) | **S2** | Manufacturer takes all measures for design/dev/production/vuln compliance. |
| CRA-CL171 | Ann. VIII Part II (1) | Module B definition | **S2** | Notified body examines design + vuln handling; attests compliance. |
| CRA-CL172 | Ann. VIII Part II (3) | Module B application | **S2** | Application to single notified body. |
| CRA-CL173 | Ann. VIII Part II (6) | EU-type certificate | **S2** | Notified body issues certificate; may refuse and inform. |
| CRA-CL174 | Ann. VIII Part II (8) | Module B periodic audits | **S2** | Periodic audits of vuln handling processes. |
| CRA-CL175 | Ann. VIII Part III (1) | Module C definition | **S2** | Manufacturer controls production per EU-type. |
| CRA-CL176 | Ann. VIII Part IV (1) | Module H definition | **S2** | Full quality assurance; notified body audits QMS. |

**Total: 9 atomic clauses for Annex VIII.**

---

The certificate shall contain the name and address of the manufacturer, the conclusions of the examination, the conditions (if any) for its validity and the necessary data for identification of the approved type and vulnerability handling processes. The certificate may have one or more annexes attached. … Where the type and the vulnerability handling processes do not satisfy the applicable essential cybersecurity requirements set out in Annex I, the notified body shall refuse to issue an EU-type examination certificate and shall inform the applicant accordingly, giving detailed reasons for its refusal.

## NIS2

#### 3.1 NIS2-CL07 — Art. 21(1) sentence 1 — Risk-management core obligation

**Source locus:**

> Member States shall ensure that essential and important entities take **<mark>appropriate and proportionate technical, operational and organisational measures</mark>** to manage the risks posed to the security of network and information systems which those entities use for their operations or for the provision of their services, and to prevent or minimise the **impact** of incidents on recipients of their services and on other services.

**Instance 1 — VAG — S3 — `appropriate and proportionate`**

The opening qualifier. Three materially distinct readings:

- **R1:** `appropriate` and `proportionate` are independent (entity must satisfy both tests, separately).
- **R2:** `appropriate` modifies `proportionate` (entity must satisfy proportionality, with `appropriate` as a placeholder synonym).
- **R3:** `appropriate and proportionate` is a single compound qualifier (a fixed phrase).

| # | Reading | Disambiguation source |
|---|---|---|
| R1 | Independent | None in OJ |
| R2 | Hierarchical | None in OJ |
| R3 | Compound | In-Directive: Art. 21(1) sentence 2 operationalises proportionality by specific factors (exposure, size, likelihood, severity) |

R1 and R3 produce materially distinct compliance obligations (R1 admits two separate failure modes; R3 admits one composite test). S3.

This is the dominant VAG trigger of NIS 2. **It propagates to all 10 sub-points of Art. 21(2)(a)–(j) and to Art. 21(3) and Art. 21(4).** Every measure listed in Art. 21(2) inherits the `appropriate and proportionate` qualifier by reference to "The measures referred to in paragraph 1".

*Berry anchor:* §5.1 (`appropriate`, `proportionate`).

**Instance 2 — POLY — S2 — `technical, operational and organisational measures`**

Three-term coordination. Three readings:

- **R1:** Three distinct categories of measures (entity must implement at least one of each).
- **R2:** Cumulative description (a measure is "technical, operational and organisational" if it has all three characteristics).
- **R3:** Open list (entity may add more categories).

| # | Reading | Disambiguation source |
|---|---|---|
| R1 | Distinct categories | In-Directive: literal `technical, operational and organisational` |
| R2 | Cumulative single measure | None in OJ |
| R3 | Open list | In-Directive: recital 56 (proportionality) |

R1 is the literal reading. R3 is the dominant reading (recital 56 implies entity-discretion on additional categories). S2.

*Berry anchor:* §5.5 (three-way coordination).

**Instance 3 — VAG + POLY — S2 — `manage the risks`**

`manage the risks` — what counts as `management`? Reading:

- **R1:** Identify + assess + mitigate.
- **R2:** Treat (avoid, accept, transfer, mitigate).
- **R3:** Continuous monitoring + adjustment.

| # | Reading | Disambiguation source |
|---|---|---|
| R1 | Identify-assess-mitigate | None in OJ; ISO/IEC 27005 |
| R2 | ISO 27005 risk-treatment | None in OJ; ISO/IEC 27005 |
| R3 | Continuous monitoring | None in OJ; NIST RMF |

*Berry anchor:* §3.3.1 (`manage`).

**Instance 4 — POLY — S2 — `impact`**

`impact` — on recipients of services (downstream effect) or on other services (cascading effect)?

- **R1:** Impact on direct recipients.
- **R2:** Impact on other services (cascade).
- **R3:** Both.

| # | Reading | Disambiguation source |
|---|---|---|
| R1 | Recipients | In-Directive: literal `recipients of their services` |
| R2 | Other services | In-Directive: literal `on other services` |
| R3 | Both | In-Directive: literal `recipients … and … other services` |

R3 is the literal reading (coordinated by `and`). The fact that the two objects are coordinated by AND rather than OR means the entity must consider impact on both. S2 because the surface ambiguity is between "focus on direct recipients" and "consider cascading effects".

*Berry anchor:* §3.3.1 (`impact`); §5.4.7 (AND).

---

#### 3.16 NIS2-CL22 — Art. 21(4) — Corrective measures

**Source locus:**

> Member States shall ensure that an entity that finds that it does not comply with the measures provided for in paragraph 2 takes, **<mark>without undue delay</mark>**, all **<mark>necessary, appropriate and proportionate corrective measures</mark>**.

**Instance 1 — VAG — S3 — `without undue delay`**

Same Berry-classic vague term as GDPR Art. 33/34 and CRA Art. 14. Three materially distinct readings:

- **R1:** `without undue delay` = 24 hours.
- **R2:** `without undue delay` = 72 hours.
- **R3:** `without undue delay` = reasonable time considering the nature of the non-compliance.

| # | Reading | Disambiguation source |
|---|---|---|
| R1 | 24 h | None in OJ |
| R2 | 72 h | None in OJ |
| R3 | Reasonable | In-Directive: standard EU drafting convention; recital 60 |

R3 is the dominant reading. R1 and R2 are Member-State-implementation divergences. S3.

*Berry anchor:* §5.1 (`undue`).

**Instance 2 — VAG — S3 — `necessary, appropriate and proportionate corrective measures`**

Triple-vague qualifier. The three adjectives (necessary/appropriate/proportionate) are coordinated by AND. The same Berry pattern as the opening of Art. 21(1). Each modifier is open; the combination admits ≥3 readings:

- **R1:** All three are independent (entity must satisfy all three tests).
- **R2:** All three are synonyms (compound description).
- **R3:** `necessary` is a floor, `appropriate` is a target, `proportionate` is a ceiling.

| # | Reading | Disambiguation source |
|---|---|---|
| R1 | Independent | None in OJ |
| R2 | Synonyms | None in OJ |
| R3 | Floor/target/ceiling | None in OJ |

R1 is the literal reading. S3.

*Berry anchor:* §5.1 (`necessary`, `appropriate`, `proportionate`).

**Instance 3 — POLY — S2 — `corrective measures`**

- **R1:** `corrective measures` = fixes to the non-compliant measure.
- **R2:** `corrective measures` = compensating controls.
- **R3:** `corrective measures` = root-cause remediation.

| # | Reading | Disambiguation source |
|---|---|---|
| R1 | Fixes | In-Directive: literal |
| R2 | Compensating | None in OJ |
| R3 | Root-cause | None in OJ; ISO/IEC 27001 corrective-action concept |

---

### 4. Cross-clause structural observations (Art. 21)

1. **Art. 21(1) opening is the single most consequential VAG phrase.** `appropriate and proportionate` propagates to every sub-point of Art. 21(2)(a)–(j), to Art. 21(3), and to Art. 21(4). Every clause in Art. 21 inherits S3 VAG on this opening qualifier. **This is structurally identical to GDPR Art. 32(1) opening's propagation to Art. 32(1)(a)–(d), and to CRA Annex I Part I §2 opening.**

2. **Art. 21(2) is structured as a 10-item list, but each item is a single phrase or short clause.** The operational content is delegated almost entirely to standards and national implementation. This makes Layer 1 findings uniformly S2-S3 — the text is too thin to generate the dense coordination ambiguities seen in GDPR Art. 4 (which has 26 definitions) or in some CRA Annex I items (which have longer compound requirements).

3. **Three sub-points of Art. 21(2) coordinate three distinct security domains in a single phrase** ((c), (i), (j)). Berry §5.5 parallelism: the structural symmetry invites the reading that each domain is an independent obligation, but the clause does not signal whether partial compliance is acceptable. **This is the dominant S3 pattern in Art. 21.**

4. **`state-of-the-art` propagation is the cross-regulation Berry-classic.** Same term in GDPR Art. 25(1) and Art. 32(1) and NIS 2 Art. 21(1) sentence 2. The three regulations have begun to diverge in national transposition: German BSI standardises the term, French ANSSI requires independent assessment, Italian ACN publishes annual guidance. The Directive's silence on the term is the **single most actionable gap for harmonisation**.

5. **`where appropriate` is the soft-hedge pattern of Art. 21.** Three sub-points are closed by `where appropriate` (h, j — and the chapeau of (j)). The hedge softens the obligation without specifying the threshold. **Cross-regulation comparison:** GDPR Art. 32(1) `where appropriate` and CRA Art. 13(5) `as appropriate` follow the same pattern.

6. **`direct` in Art. 21(2)(d) is the single most consequential scope-limiter.** The reading determines whether the obligation reaches 1-hop, n-hop, or only contractual counter-parties. The Directive is silent; the Commission's 17 October 2024 implementing acts (Art. 21(5)) were expected to clarify this but as of mid-2025 are not yet published.

7. **T3-vs-text gaps are the most extensive in the corpus so far.** At least 6 sub-points (b, d, e, f, g, h, i, j) are split by T3 into multiple T3 clauses that the OJ text does not support. The at-rest/in-transit/key-management split (h) is the largest single gap: three T3 clauses on one OJ sub-point.

8. **The "physical environment" scope extension is the most distinctive feature of NIS 2 vs other regulations in the corpus.** All other regulations (GDPR, CRA, DORA, AI Act) are software/cyber-focused. NIS 2's all-hazards + physical-environment coverage is the unique outlier. **For software-compliance analysis, the physical-environment dimension is out-of-scope** — it does not generate software requirements.

---

#### 3.3 NIS2-CL28 — Art. 23(1) ¶1 — Cross-border information

**Source locus:**

> Each Member State shall ensure that those entities report, inter alia, any information enabling the CSIRT or, where applicable, the competent authority to determine any cross-border impact of the incident.

**Instance 1 — VAG — S2 — `inter alia`**

`inter alia` — among other things. The reporting is non-exhaustive; the entity may report more than the listed items. Standard EU drafting; the cross-border-impact info is mandatory, but other info is permitted.

*Berry anchor:* §3.3.1 (`inter alia`).

**Instance 2 — VAG — S2 — `cross-border impact`**

`cross-border impact` — impact in how many Member States? Impact of what kind?

- **R1:** Impact in two or more Member States.
- **R2:** Impact on cross-border services (single MS but cross-border flow).
- **R3:** Either.

| # | Reading | Disambiguation source |
|---|---|---|
| R1 | ≥2 MS | In-Directive: Art. 6(7) `large-scale cybersecurity incident` |
| R2 | Cross-border services | None in OJ |
| R3 | Either | None in OJ |

R1 is the literal reading. S2.

*Berry anchor:* §3.3.1 (`cross-border`); §5.2.1 (`cross-border`).
